========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well… I guess what finally made me come in is my head just doesn’t feel clear. It’s like I’m trying to do my normal work — looking at a board packet, or reading through a vendor email — and I just sit there staring at it. I used to be able to sort through those things pretty quickly, make a decision, move on to the next thing. Now even simple things feel like they take forever, and I keep thinking, “Why can’t I just decide this?”

It’s not that I don’t know the work. I’ve done this kind of thing for years. But lately it feels cloudy up there, like I’m pushing through fog, and it’s frustrating because these are things I’m supposed to handle. That’s really what brought me in.

I’m not sure I’d call it depressed, exactly. But lately it feels cloudy up there, like I’m pushing through fog, and it’s frustrating because these are things I’m supposed to handle. Yes… I think I have, now that you say it that way. I didn’t really call it that in my head, but there are things I normally look forward to that I just… haven’t wanted to do.

Like my dance class — I usually like going, even if I’m tired, because once I’m there I feel better and I know the people. Lately I’ll think about getting dressed and driving over there, and I just don’t want to. And I used to like walking around and looking at the little galleries, just seeing pretty things, getting ideas, but I haven’t had the pull to do that either.

And Sundays… I usually cook for my family. Nothing fancy, just the food we’re used to, the kind that makes the house feel right. But even that has felt like too much, or like I don’t care the same way. Those things usually make me feel like myself, so it bothers me that I’m not wanting them.

Yes… I think so. I mean, not every single minute, but most days it’s been there in the background. The biggest thing is I just haven’t wanted to do the things I normally do, the things that usually make me feel like myself.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think there have been other stretches, if I’m being honest. It’s not like this is the very first time I’ve felt this kind of heaviness or like I don’t care about things the way I usually do.

Over maybe the past year, it’s been coming and going. I’d have these periods where I’d feel down or just kind of lose my spark for a while, and then it would lift some, so I’d tell myself, “Okay, I’m fine, it passed.” But then later it would come back again.

This latest one feels a little different to me, though, or maybe more connected to what’s been going on at home. It seemed to get worse after my husband stopped working. I don’t know if that’s the cause exactly, but that’s when I noticed it more — like the whole feeling of being weighed down and not wanting to do my usual things became harder to shake. So yes, before this, there were other times, on and off, that lasted a couple weeks or longer.

And even cooking Sunday food for my family… that usually feels important to me, like part of who I am, and lately I just don’t have the same interest in doing it.\n\nSo yes, for the past couple of weeks or so, I’d say I’ve been less interested most of the day, most days.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt down or lost interest in things for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there have been other stretches, if I’m being honest. It’s not like this is the very first time I’ve felt this kind of heaviness or like I don’t care about things the way I usually do.\n\nOver maybe the past year, it’s been coming and going. I’d have these periods where I’d feel down or just kind of lose my spark for a while, and then it would lift some, so I’d tell myself, “Okay, I’m fine, it passed.” But then later it would come back again.\n\nThis latest one feels a little different to me, though, or maybe more connected to what’s been going on at home. It seemed to get worse after my husband stopped working. I don’t know if that’s the cause exactly, but that’s when I noticed it more — like the whole feeling of being weighed down and not wanting to do my usual things became harder to shake. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, that’s when I really noticed it getting sharper. I mean, I’d had ups and downs before, but this particular change — my energy dropping, not feeling confident in myself, and then this kind of low, cloudy feeling — it seemed to start after my husband retired and was suddenly home all day.

It’s been about two weeks now, maybe right around there. I don’t want to blame him exactly, because it’s not that simple, but the timing lines up. Once he was home all the time, the house felt different, my routine felt different, and that’s when I started feeling more weighed down.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d use the word depressed exactly… sad, yes, some days. It’s more like I feel low or heavy, like I’m not quite myself. I can still get up and do what I need to do, so part of me says, “Well, maybe it’s not that bad,” but there’s definitely been this sadness sitting there.

It’s not like I’m crying all day or anything like that. It’s more quiet than that. I’ll be doing something ordinary and just feel this dull, down feeling come over me. So yes, I guess I have been feeling sad during this period, but I keep going back and forth on what to call it.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF33.9, F32.9, F43.23, F41.9, F06.7, F09\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what finally made me come in is my head just doesn’t feel clear. It’s like I’m trying to do my normal work — looking at a board packet, or reading through a vendor email — and I just sit there staring at it. I used to be able to sort through those things pretty quickly, make a decision, move on to the next thing. Now even simple things feel like they take forever, and I keep thinking, “Why can’t I just decide this?”\n\nIt’s not that I don’t know the work. I’ve done this kind of thing for years. But lately it feels cloudy up there, like I’m pushing through fog, and it’s frustrating because these are things I’m supposed to handle. That’s really what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, I get upset with myself, definitely — more discouraged than anything, because I’ll sit there and think, “I should be able to do this, why is this taking me so long?” But when you say depressed, I don’t know… I guess I don’t really know where the line is between being frustrated and being down.\n\nI wouldn’t say I came in thinking, “I’m depressed.” It’s more that something feels off, and then that makes me feel bad about myself. Like I’m not keeping up the way I normally would. So… maybe a little down at times, but I’m not sure if that’s the right word.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have, now that you say it that way. I didn’t really call it that in my head, but there are things I normally look forward to that I just… haven’t wanted to do.\n\nLike my dance class — I usually like going, even if I’m tired, because once I’m there I feel better and I know the people. Lately I’ll think about getting dressed and driving over there, and I just don’t want to. And I used to like walking around and looking at the little galleries, just seeing pretty things, getting ideas, but I haven’t had the pull to do that either.\n\nAnd Sundays… I usually cook for my family. Nothing fancy, just the food we’re used to, the kind that makes the house feel right. But even that has felt like too much, or like I don’t care the same way. Those things usually make me feel like myself, so it bothers me that I’m not wanting them.\n\nDoctor: For at least the past two weeks, have you been feeling down or less interested in things most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, not every single minute, but most days it’s been there in the background. The biggest thing is I just haven’t wanted to do the things I normally do, the things that usually make me feel like myself.\n\nLike I said, I haven’t wanted to go to my dance class, and that’s strange for me because I usually enjoy it once I’m there. I haven’t felt like going to look at the little galleries either, which is something I used to do just because it made me feel good. And even cooking Sunday food for my family… that usually feels important to me, like part of who I am, and lately I just don’t have the same interest in doing it.\n\nSo yes, for the past couple of weeks or so, I’d say I’ve been less interested most of the day, most days.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt down or lost interest in things for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there have been other stretches, if I’m being honest. It’s not like this is the very first time I’ve felt this kind of heaviness or like I don’t care about things the way I usually do.\n\nOver maybe the past year, it’s been coming and going. I’d have these periods where I’d feel down or just kind of lose my spark for a while, and then it would lift some, so I’d tell myself, “Okay, I’m fine, it passed.” But then later it would come back again.\n\nThis latest one feels a little different to me, though, or maybe more connected to what’s been going on at home. It seemed to get worse after my husband stopped working. I don’t know if that’s the cause exactly, but that’s when I noticed it more — like the whole feeling of being weighed down and not wanting to do my usual things became harder to shake. So yes, before this, there were other times, on and off, that lasted a couple weeks or longer.\n\nDoctor: Did these symptoms start after your husband stopped working?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s when I really noticed it getting sharper. I don’t want to blame him exactly, because it’s not that simple, but the timing lines up. Once he was home all the time, the house felt different, my routine felt different, and that’s when I started feeling more weighed down.\n\nDoctor: During this current period, have you been feeling sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d use the word depressed exactly… sad, yes, some days. It’s more like I feel low or heavy, like I’m not quite myself. So yes, I guess I have been feeling sad during this period, but I keep going back and forth on what to call it.\n\n[Last Question]\nSince your husband stopped working, has this been interfering with your day-to-day functioning?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 64 / property_real_estate_or_community_association_manager / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I am still getting to work, but I have had to slow down decisions, ask my assistant to double-check vendor details, and put off a couple of board items because I do not trust my judgment right now.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I mean, I’m still functioning, if that’s what you mean. I’m still getting myself to work, I’m not staying in bed or anything like that. But I can tell I’m not operating the way I usually do.

At work, I’ve been slowing myself down a lot more before making decisions. Normally I can look at something, sort through it, and decide what needs to be done, especially with vendors or board issues. Lately I keep second-guessing myself, like, “Did I read that right? Am I missing something?” So I’ve been asking my assistant to double-check vendor details more than I normally would, because I just don’t trust my judgment right now.

And there are a couple of board items I’ve put off, not because they’re impossible, but because I feel like if I make the call right now, I might make the wrong one. So yes, it is interfering some. Not to the point that everything has fallen apart, but enough that I notice I’m not doing my job with the same confidence or speed.

Like I’m not keeping up the way I normally would. I didn’t really call it that in my head, but there are things I normally look forward to that I just… haven’t wanted to do.\n\nLike my dance class — I usually like going, even if I’m tired, because once I’m there I feel better and I know the people. Lately I’ll think about getting dressed and driving over there, and I just don’t want to. I haven’t felt like going to look at the little galleries either, which is something I used to do just because it made me feel good. I’d have these periods where I’d feel down or just kind of lose my spark for a while, and then it would lift some, so I’d tell myself, “Okay, I’m fine, it passed.” But then later it would come back again.\n\nThis latest one feels a little different to me, though, or maybe more connected to what’s been going on at home. Once he was home all the time, the house felt different, my routine felt different, and that’s when I started feeling more weighed down.\n\nDoctor: During this current period, have you been feeling sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d use the word depressed exactly… sad, yes, some days. It’s more like I feel low or heavy, like I’m not quite myself. I can still get up and do what I need to do, so part of me says, “Well, maybe it’s not that bad,” but there’s definitely been this sadness sitting there.\n\nIt’s not like I’m crying all day or anything like that. It’s more quiet than that. I’ll be doing something ordinary and just feel this dull, down feeling come over me. So yes, I guess I have been feeling sad during this period, but I keep going back and forth on what to call it.\n\nDoctor: Since your husband stopped working, has this been interfering with your day-to-day functioning?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I’m still functioning, if that’s what you mean. I’m still getting myself to work, I’m not staying in bed or anything like that. But I can tell I’m not operating the way I usually do.\n\nAt work, I’ve been slowing myself down a lot more before making decisions. Normally I can look at something, sort through it, and decide what needs to be done, especially with vendors or board issues. Lately I keep second-guessing myself, like, “Did I read that right? Am I missing something?” So I’ve been asking my assistant to double-check vendor details more than I normally would, because I just don’t trust my judgment right now.\n\nAnd there are a couple of board items I’ve put off, not because they’re impossible, but because I feel like if I make the call right now, I might make the wrong one. So yes, it is interfering some. Not to the point that everything has fallen apart, but enough that I notice I’m not doing my job with the same confidence or speed.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling especially worried or anxious lately?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 64 / property_real_estate_or_community_association_manager / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d say especially anxious. I mean, I do worry, but I’ve always been someone who thinks things through, maybe too much sometimes. Lately I notice myself turning things over in my head more, but I don’t know if that’s anxiety exactly or just me not feeling as steady as I usually do.

It’s not like I’m sitting there panicking, if that’s what you mean. It’s more… I’ll pause and think, “Am I handling this right?” or “Should I check that again?” But I wouldn’t say I feel scared all the time or anything that strong. So I guess my answer is, maybe a little worried, but I’m not sure I’d call it especially anxious.

Lately I notice myself turning things over in my head more, but I don’t know if that’s anxiety exactly or just me not feeling as steady as I usually do.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there panicking, if that’s what you mean. It’s more… I’ll pause and think, “Am I handling this right?” or “Should I check that again?” But I wouldn’t say I feel scared all the time or anything that strong. So I guess my answer is, maybe a little worried, but I’m not sure I’d call it especially anxious.\n\n[Last Question]\nBefore your husband retired, was there ever a time when you felt this same kind of low mood or loss of interest nearly every day for two weeks or longer?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… before he stopped working, I think there were times like that, though I’m not sure I was naming it that way at the time. It’s been kind of coming and going for maybe about a year now, these stretches where I feel low and sort of pulled back from things, not just for a day here or there.

Some of those periods did last a couple of weeks, I think. It’s not always dramatic, so I can talk myself out of calling it anything serious, but looking back, yes, there were stretches where it was there nearly every day.

This latest one feels more connected to my husband not working anymore, like that change stirred it up or made it more noticeable. But the low patches themselves didn’t start only after that. They’ve been on and off before then.

Like I’m not keeping up the way I normally would. I didn’t really call it that in my head, but there are things I normally look forward to that I just… haven’t wanted to do.\n\nLike my dance class — I usually like going, even if I’m tired, because once I’m there I feel better and I know the people. Lately I’ll think about getting dressed and driving over there, and I just don’t want to. Nothing fancy, just the food we’re used to, the kind that makes the house feel right. But even that has felt like too much, or like I don’t care the same way. Those things usually make me feel like myself, so it bothers me that I’m not wanting them.\n\nDoctor: For at least the past two weeks, have you been feeling down or less interested in things most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. the scary thought that maybe it would be easier if I just did not wake up, but I do not want to hurt myself and I have no plan.\n- key: S033\n  name: Elevated_Irritable_Mood\n  description: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.\n  manifestation: There were days when I felt almost too bright and bold, talking over people at the association meetings, and then I would snap at my husband over some small thing at home.\n- key: S034\n  name: Increased_Activity\n  description: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.\n  manifestation: I had this push in my body like I had to keep moving, calling people, making lists, and walking the properties even after I should have gone home.\n- key: S035\n  name: Inflated_Self_Esteem\n  description: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).\n  manifestation: I started feeling like I could fix every building problem in Denver if people would just let me run with my ideas, which is not how I usually talk about myself.\n- key: S038\n  name: Racing_Thoughts\n  description: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.\n  manifestation: My thoughts have been jumping so fast from solar panels to budgets to my husband being home that I cannot catch one idea long enough to finish it.\n- key: S040\n  name: Increased_Goal_Activity\n  description: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.\n  manifestation: I took on extra green-energy proposals for several communities at the same time and kept setting up calls and meetings even when my calendar was already full.\n- key: S041\n  name: Painful_Consequence_Behavior\n  description: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.\n  manifestation: I told a couple of vendors to go ahead and hold equipment or start paperwork before I had really checked the budgets, and now I am worried I put us in a bad spot.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I am still getting to work, but I have had to slow down decisions, ask my assistant to double-check vendor details, and put off a couple of board items because I do not trust my judgment right now.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 weeks\n  manifestation: This sharper change in my energy, confidence, and then the low cloudy feeling has been going on for about two weeks, since my husband retired and was suddenly home all day.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 1 year\n  manifestation: The depressed stretches have been coming back on and off for about a year, but this latest one feels more tied to what happened after my husband stopped working.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 64 / property_real_estate_or_community_association_manager / less_than_9th\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S031\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Since your husband stopped working, has this been interfering with your day-to-day functioning?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I’m still functioning, if that’s what you mean. I’m still getting myself to work, I’m not staying in bed or anything like that. But I can tell I’m not operating the way I usually do.\n\nAt work, I’ve been slowing myself down a lot more before making decisions. Normally I can look at something, sort through it, and decide what needs to be done, especially with vendors or board issues. Lately I keep second-guessing myself, like, “Did I read that right? Am I missing something?” So I’ve been asking my assistant to double-check vendor details more than I normally would, because I just don’t trust my judgment right now.\n\nAnd there are a couple of board items I’ve put off, not because they’re impossible, but because I feel like if I make the call right now, I might make the wrong one. So yes, it is interfering some. Not to the point that everything has fallen apart, but enough that I notice I’m not doing my job with the same confidence or speed.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling especially worried or anxious lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say especially anxious. I mean, I do worry, but I’ve always been someone who thinks things through, maybe too much sometimes. Lately I notice myself turning things over in my head more, but I don’t know if that’s anxiety exactly or just me not feeling as steady as I usually do.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there panicking, if that’s what you mean. It’s more… I’ll pause and think, “Am I handling this right?” or “Should I check that again?” But I wouldn’t say I feel scared all the time or anything that strong. So I guess my answer is, maybe a little worried, but I’m not sure I’d call it especially anxious.\n\nDoctor: Before your husband retired, was there ever a time when you felt this same kind of low mood or loss of interest nearly every day for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: Yes… before he stopped working, I think there were times like that, though I’m not sure I was naming it that way at the time. It’s been kind of coming and going for maybe about a year now, these stretches where I feel low and sort of pulled back from things, not just for a day here or there.\n\nSome of those periods did last a couple of weeks, I think. It’s not always dramatic, so I can talk myself out of calling it anything serious, but looking back, yes, there were stretches where it was there nearly every day.\n\nThis latest one feels more connected to my husband not working anymore, like that change stirred it up or made it more noticeable. But the low patches themselves didn’t start only after that. They’ve been on and off before then.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. I’m not really sure how to answer that, because I haven’t been tracking it closely. I don’t think I’ve noticed a major problem, like something obvious where I’d say, “Oh, I’m not sleeping at all,” or anything like that.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what finally made me come in is my head just doesn’t feel clear. It’s like I’m trying to do my normal work — looking at a board packet, or reading through a vendor email — and I just sit there staring at it. I used to be able to sort through those things pretty quickly, make a decision, move on to the next thing. Now even simple things feel like they take forever, and I keep thinking, “Why can’t I just decide this?”\n\nIt’s not that I don’t know the work. I’ve done this kind of thing for years. But lately it feels cloudy up there, like I’m pushing through fog, and it’s frustrating because these are things I’m supposed to handle. That’s really what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, I get upset with myself, definitely — more discouraged than anything, because I’ll sit there and think, “I should be able to do this, why is this taking me so long?” But when you say depressed, I don’t know… I guess I don’t really know where the line is between being frustrated and being down.\n\nI wouldn’t say I came in thinking, “I’m depressed.” It’s more that something feels off, and then that makes me feel bad about myself. Like I’m not keeping up the way I normally would. So… maybe a little down at times, but I’m not sure if that’s the right word.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have, now that you say it that way. I didn’t really call it that in my head, but there are things I normally look forward to that I just… haven’t wanted to do.\n\nLike my dance class — I usually like going, even if I’m tired, because once I’m there I feel better and I know the people. Lately I’ll think about getting dressed and driving over there, and I just don’t want to. And I used to like walking around and looking at the little galleries, just seeing pretty things, getting ideas, but I haven’t had the pull to do that either.\n\nAnd Sundays… I usually cook for my family. Nothing fancy, just the food we’re used to, the kind that makes the house feel right. But even that has felt like too much, or like I don’t care the same way. Those things usually make me feel like myself, so it bothers me that I’m not wanting them.\n\nDoctor: For at least the past two weeks, have you been feeling down or less interested in things most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, not every single minute, but most days it’s been there in the background. The biggest thing is I just haven’t wanted to do the things I normally do, the things that usually make me feel like myself.\n\nLike I said, I haven’t wanted to go to my dance class, and that’s strange for me because I usually enjoy it once I’m there. I haven’t felt like going to look at the little galleries either, which is something I used to do just because it made me feel good. And even cooking Sunday food for my family… that usually feels important to me, like part of who I am, and lately I just don’t have the same interest in doing it.\n\nSo yes, for the past couple of weeks or so, I’d say I’ve been less interested most of the day, most days.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt down or lost interest in things for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there have been other stretches, if I’m being honest. It’s not like this is the very first time I’ve felt this kind of heaviness or like I don’t care about things the way I usually do.\n\nOver maybe the past year, it’s been coming and going. I’d have these periods where I’d feel down or just kind of lose my spark for a while, and then it would lift some, so I’d tell myself, “Okay, I’m fine, it passed.” But then later it would come back again.\n\nThis latest one feels a little different to me, though, or maybe more connected to what’s been going on at home. It seemed to get worse after my husband stopped working. I don’t know if that’s the cause exactly, but that’s when I noticed it more — like the whole feeling of being weighed down and not wanting to do my usual things became harder to shake. So yes, before this, there were other times, on and off, that lasted a couple weeks or longer.\n\nDoctor: Did these symptoms start after your husband stopped working?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s when I really noticed it getting sharper. I mean, I’d had ups and downs before, but this particular change — my energy dropping, not feeling confident in myself, and then this kind of low, cloudy feeling — it seemed to start after my husband retired and was suddenly home all day.\n\nIt’s been about two weeks now, maybe right around there. I don’t want to blame him exactly, because it’s not that simple, but the timing lines up. Once he was home all the time, the house felt different, my routine felt different, and that’s when I started feeling more weighed down.\n\nDoctor: During this current period, have you been feeling sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d use the word depressed exactly… sad, yes, some days. It’s more like I feel low or heavy, like I’m not quite myself. I can still get up and do what I need to do, so part of me says, “Well, maybe it’s not that bad,” but there’s definitely been this sadness sitting there.\n\nIt’s not like I’m crying all day or anything like that. It’s more quiet than that. I’ll be doing something ordinary and just feel this dull, down feeling come over me. So yes, I guess I have been feeling sad during this period, but I keep going back and forth on what to call it.\n\nDoctor: Since your husband stopped working, has this been interfering with your day-to-day functioning?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I’m still functioning, if that’s what you mean. I’m still getting myself to work, I’m not staying in bed or anything like that. But I can tell I’m not operating the way I usually do.\n\nAt work, I’ve been slowing myself down a lot more before making decisions. Normally I can look at something, sort through it, and decide what needs to be done, especially with vendors or board issues. Lately I keep second-guessing myself, like, “Did I read that right? Am I missing something?” So I’ve been asking my assistant to double-check vendor details more than I normally would, because I just don’t trust my judgment right now.\n\nAnd there are a couple of board items I’ve put off, not because they’re impossible, but because I feel like if I make the call right now, I might make the wrong one. So yes, it is interfering some. Not to the point that everything has fallen apart, but enough that I notice I’m not doing my job with the same confidence or speed.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling especially worried or anxious lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say especially anxious. They’ve been on and off before then.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that, because I haven’t been tracking it closely. Memory… I don’t know if I’d call it memory exactly, like forgetting people’s names or not knowing where I am. It’s more that my head feels cloudy. That’s actually a lot of why I came in.

I’ll be looking at a board packet, or a vendor email, something I used to go through pretty quickly, and I just sort of stare at it. I read the same part again and again, and then I still can’t seem to decide what to do with it. Even simple things, like whether something needs a call back or whether I should approve a small thing, feel harder than they should.

It makes me feel foolish, honestly, because these are things I’ve handled for years. It’s not that I don’t know how, exactly. It’s like the thinking part is slow or foggy, and I can’t get myself to land on an answer. So if that counts as memory trouble, then yes, but to me it feels more like trouble thinking clearly.

I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, I don’t think. I haven’t been missing appointments, at least not that I can remember, and I don’t have people telling me, “We already talked about this,” or anything like that.

Sometimes I’ll second-guess myself, like I’ll think, did I already call that person back, or did I just mean to call them? But then if I look at my notes or my calendar, usually I can sort it out. I’ve always kept lists and written things down for work, so it’s hard to know how much is memory and how much is just me relying on my usual system.

With conversations, I might not remember every little detail, especially if I wasn’t fully focused, but I don’t think I’m forgetting whole conversations. It’s more that I feel unsure of myself and have to check things more than I used to.

The biggest thing is I just haven’t wanted to do the things I normally do, the things that usually make me feel like myself.\n\nLike I said, I haven’t wanted to go to my dance class, and that’s strange for me because I usually enjoy it once I’m there. I haven’t felt like going to look at the little galleries either, which is something I used to do just because it made me feel good. And even cooking Sunday food for my family… that usually feels important to me, like part of who I am, and lately I just don’t have the same interest in doing it.\n\nSo yes, for the past couple of weeks or so, I’d say I’ve been less interested most of the day, most days.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt down or lost interest in things for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there have been other stretches, if I’m being honest. It’s not like this is the very first time I’ve felt this kind of heaviness or like I don’t care about things the way I usually do.\n\nOver maybe the past year, it’s been coming and going. I’d have these periods where I’d feel down or just kind of lose my spark for a while, and then it would lift some, so I’d tell myself, “Okay, I’m fine, it passed.” But then later it would come back again.\n\nThis latest one feels a little different to me, though, or maybe more connected to what’s been going on at home. It seemed to get worse after my husband stopped working. I don’t know if that’s the cause exactly, but that’s when I noticed it more — like the whole feeling of being weighed down and not wanting to do my usual things became harder to shake. So yes, before this, there were other times, on and off, that lasted a couple weeks or longer.\n\nDoctor: Did these symptoms start after your husband stopped working?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s when I really noticed it getting sharper. I mean, I’d had ups and downs before, but this particular change — my energy dropping, not feeling confident in myself, and then this kind of low, cloudy feeling — it seemed to start after my husband retired and was suddenly home all day.\n\nIt’s been about two weeks now, maybe right around there. I don’t want to blame him exactly, because it’s not that simple, but the timing lines up. Once he was home all the time, the house felt different, my routine felt different, and that’s when I started feeling more weighed down.\n\nDoctor: During this current period, have you been feeling sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d use the word depressed exactly… sad, yes, some days. It’s more like I feel low or heavy, like I’m not quite myself. I can still get up and do what I need to do, so part of me says, “Well, maybe it’s not that bad,” but there’s definitely been this sadness sitting there.\n\nIt’s not like I’m crying all day or anything like that. It’s more quiet than that. I’ll be doing something ordinary and just feel this dull, down feeling come over me. So yes, I guess I have been feeling sad during this period, but I keep going back and forth on what to call it.\n\nDoctor: Since your husband stopped working, has this been interfering with your day-to-day functioning?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I’m still functioning, if that’s what you mean. I’m still getting myself to work, I’m not staying in bed or anything like that. But I can tell I’m not operating the way I usually do.\n\nAt work, I’ve been slowing myself down a lot more before making decisions. Normally I can look at something, sort through it, and decide what needs to be done, especially with vendors or board issues. Lately I keep second-guessing myself, like, “Did I read that right? Am I missing something?” So I’ve been asking my assistant to double-check vendor details more than I normally would, because I just don’t trust my judgment right now.\n\nAnd there are a couple of board items I’ve put off, not because they’re impossible, but because I feel like if I make the call right now, I might make the wrong one. So yes, it is interfering some. Not to the point that everything has fallen apart, but enough that I notice I’m not doing my job with the same confidence or speed.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling especially worried or anxious lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say especially anxious. I mean, I do worry, but I’ve always been someone who thinks things through, maybe too much sometimes. Lately I notice myself turning things over in my head more, but I don’t know if that’s anxiety exactly or just me not feeling as steady as I usually do.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there panicking, if that’s what you mean. It’s more… I’ll pause and think, “Am I handling this right?” or “Should I check that again?” But I wouldn’t say I feel scared all the time or anything that strong. So I guess my answer is, maybe a little worried, but I’m not sure I’d call it especially anxious.\n\nDoctor: Before your husband retired, was there ever a time when you felt this same kind of low mood or loss of interest nearly every day for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: Yes… before he stopped working, I think there were times like that, though I’m not sure I was naming it that way at the time. It’s been kind of coming and going for maybe about a year now, these stretches where I feel low and sort of pulled back from things, not just for a day here or there.\n\nSome of those periods did last a couple of weeks, I think. It’s not always dramatic, so I can talk myself out of calling it anything serious, but looking back, yes, there were stretches where it was there nearly every day.\n\nThis latest one feels more connected to my husband not working anymore, like that change stirred it up or made it more noticeable. But the low patches themselves didn’t start only after that. They’ve been on and off before then.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that, because I haven’t been tracking it closely. I don’t think I’ve noticed a major problem, like something obvious where I’d say, “Oh, I’m not sleeping at all,” or anything like that.\n\nSome nights I might wake up or not feel completely rested, but I don’t know if that’s unusual for me or just normal at my age, or because I’m thinking about things before bed. I wouldn’t say sleep is the main thing I’ve been concerned about. If you mean trouble falling asleep every night or being up for hours, I don’t think so… at least not that I’ve really noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory?\n\nPatient: Memory… I don’t know if I’d call it memory exactly, like forgetting people’s names or not knowing where I am. It’s more that my head feels cloudy. That’s actually a lot of why I came in.\n\nI’ll be looking at a board packet, or a vendor email, something I used to go through pretty quickly, and I just sort of stare at it. I read the same part again and again, and then I still can’t seem to decide what to do with it. Even simple things, like whether something needs a call back or whether I should approve a small thing, feel harder than they should.\n\nIt makes me feel foolish, honestly, because these are things I’ve handled for years. It’s not that I don’t know how, exactly. It’s like the thinking part is slow or foggy, and I can’t get myself to land on an answer. So if that counts as memory trouble, then yes, but to me it feels more like trouble thinking clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been forgetting recent conversations or appointments?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, I don’t think. I haven’t been missing appointments, at least not that I can remember, and I don’t have people telling me, “We already talked about this,” or anything like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll second-guess myself, like I’ll think, did I already call that person back, or did I just mean to call them? But then if I look at my notes or my calendar, usually I can sort it out. I’ve always kept lists and written things down for work, so it’s hard to know how much is memory and how much is just me relying on my usual system.\n\nWith conversations, I might not remember every little detail, especially if I wasn’t fully focused, but I don’t think I’m forgetting whole conversations. It’s more that I feel unsure of myself and have to check things more than I used to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Bipolar II Disorder, in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that, because I haven’t been tracking it closely. I don’t think I’ve noticed a major problem, like something obvious where I’d say, “Oh, I’m not sleeping at all,” or anything like that.\n\nSome nights I might wake up or not feel completely rested, but I don’t know if that’s unusual for me or just normal at my age, or because I’m thinking about things before bed. I wouldn’t say sleep is the main thing I’ve been concerned about. If you mean trouble falling asleep every night or being up for hours, I don’t think so… at least not that I’ve really noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory?\n\nPatient: Memory… I don’t know if I’d call it memory exactly, like forgetting people’s names or not knowing where I am. It’s more that my head feels cloudy. That’s actually a lot of why I came in.\n\nI’ll be looking at a board packet, or a vendor email, something I used to go through pretty quickly, and I just sort of stare at it. I read the same part again and again, and then I still can’t seem to decide what to do with it. Even simple things, like whether something needs a call back or whether I should approve a small thing, feel harder than they should.\n\nIt makes me feel foolish, honestly, because these are things I’ve handled for years. It’s not that I don’t know how, exactly. It’s like the thinking part is slow or foggy, and I can’t get myself to land on an answer. So if that counts as memory trouble, then yes, but to me it feels more like trouble thinking clearly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been forgetting recent conversations or appointments?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, I don’t think. I haven’t been missing appointments, at least not that I can remember, and I don’t have people telling me, “We already talked about this,” or anything like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll second-guess myself, like I’ll think, did I already call that person back, or did I just mean to call them? But then if I look at my notes or my calendar, usually I can sort it out. I’ve always kept lists and written things down for work, so it’s hard to know how much is memory and how much is just me relying on my usual system.\n\nWith conversations, I might not remember every little detail, especially if I wasn’t fully focused, but I don’t think I’m forgetting whole conversations. It’s more that I feel unsure of myself and have to check things more than I used to.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed your appetite has gone down lately?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 64 / property_real_estate_or_community_association_manager / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Weight_Appetite_Change\nmanifestation: \"My eating has been off, where coffee and a few bites carry me most of the day and then at night I pick at tortillas or sweets without really enjoying them.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think it has, though it’s kind of uneven. It’s not like I never eat, but my eating has been off. A lot of days I’ll have coffee, and then maybe just a few bites of something, and somehow I just keep going like that for most of the day.

Then later at night I’ll realize I didn’t really eat properly, but even then I’m not sitting down to a real meal. I’ll just pick at something, like tortillas or something sweet, a little here and there. And I don’t even enjoy it much, which is strange for me. It’s more like I’m eating because it’s there, or because I know I should have something, not because I’m hungry.

So yes, I guess my appetite is down, or at least it doesn’t feel normal.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF33.9, F32.9, F43.23, F41.9, F06.7, F09\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what finally made me come in is my head just doesn’t feel clear. It’s like I’m trying to do my normal work — looking at a board packet, or reading through a vendor email — and I just sit there staring at it. I used to be able to sort through those things pretty quickly, make a decision, move on to the next thing. Now even simple things feel like they take forever, and I keep thinking, “Why can’t I just decide this?”\n\nIt’s not that I don’t know the work. I’ve done this kind of thing for years. But lately it feels cloudy up there, like I’m pushing through fog, and it’s frustrating because these are things I’m supposed to handle. That’s really what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, I get upset with myself, definitely — more discouraged than anything, because I’ll sit there and think, “I should be able to do this, why is this taking me so long?” But when you say depressed, I don’t know… I guess I don’t really know where the line is between being frustrated and being down.\n\nI wouldn’t say I came in thinking, “I’m depressed.” It’s more that something feels off, and then that makes me feel bad about myself. Like I’m not keeping up the way I normally would. So… maybe a little down at times, but I’m not sure if that’s the right word.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have, now that you say it that way. I didn’t really call it that in my head, but there are things I normally look forward to that I just… haven’t wanted to do.\n\nLike my dance class — I usually like going, even if I’m tired, because once I’m there I feel better and I know the people. Lately I’ll think about getting dressed and driving over there, and I just don’t want to. And I used to like walking around and looking at the little galleries, just seeing pretty things, getting ideas, but I haven’t had the pull to do that either.\n\nAnd Sundays… I usually cook for my family. Nothing fancy, just the food we’re used to, the kind that makes the house feel right. But even that has felt like too much, or like I don’t care the same way. Those things usually make me feel like myself, so it bothers me that I’m not wanting them.\n\nDoctor: For at least the past two weeks, have you been feeling down or less interested in things most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, not every single minute, but most days it’s been there in the background. The biggest thing is I just haven’t wanted to do the things I normally do, the things that usually make me feel like myself.\n\nLike I said, I haven’t wanted to go to my dance class, and that’s strange for me because I usually enjoy it once I’m there. I haven’t felt like going to look at the little galleries either, which is something I used to do just because it made me feel good. And even cooking Sunday food for my family… that usually feels important to me, like part of who I am, and lately I just don’t have the same interest in doing it.\n\nSo yes, for the past couple of weeks or so, I’d say I’ve been less interested most of the day, most days.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt down or lost interest in things for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there have been other stretches, if I’m being honest. It’s not like this is the very first time I’ve felt this kind of heaviness or like I don’t care about things the way I usually do.\n\nOver maybe the past year, it’s been coming and going. I’d have these periods where I’d feel down or just kind of lose my spark for a while, and then it would lift some, so I’d tell myself, “Okay, I’m fine, it passed.” But then later it would come back again.\n\nThis latest one feels a little different to me, though, or maybe more connected to what’s been going on at home. It seemed to get worse after my husband stopped working. I don’t know if that’s the cause exactly, but that’s when I noticed it more — like the whole feeling of being weighed down and not wanting to do my usual things became harder to shake. So yes, before this, there were other times, on and off, that lasted a couple weeks or longer.\n\nDoctor: Did these symptoms start after your husband stopped working?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s when I really noticed it getting sharper. I mean, I’d had ups and downs before, but this particular change — my energy dropping, not feeling confident in myself, and then this kind of low, cloudy feeling — it seemed to start after my husband retired and was suddenly home all day.\n\nIt’s been about two weeks now, maybe right around there. I don’t want to blame him exactly, because it’s not that simple, but the timing lines up. Once he was home all the time, the house felt different, my routine felt different, and that’s when I started feeling more weighed down.\n\nDoctor: During this current period, have you been feeling sad or depressed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d use the word depressed exactly… sad, yes, some days. It’s more like I feel low or heavy, like I’m not quite myself. I can still get up and do what I need to do, so part of me says, “Well, maybe it’s not that bad,” but there’s definitely been this sadness sitting there.\n\nIt’s not like I’m crying all day or anything like that. It’s more quiet than that. I’ll be doing something ordinary and just feel this dull, down feeling come over me. So yes, I guess I have been feeling sad during this period, but I keep going back and forth on what to call it.\n\nDoctor: Since your husband stopped working, has this been interfering with your day-to-day functioning?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I’m still functioning, if that’s what you mean. I’m still getting myself to work, I’m not staying in bed or anything like that. But I can tell I’m not operating the way I usually do.\n\nAt work, I’ve been slowing myself down a lot more before making decisions. Normally I can look at something, sort through it, and decide what needs to be done, especially with vendors or board issues. Lately I keep second-guessing myself, like, “Did I read that right? Am I missing something?” So I’ve been asking my assistant to double-check vendor details more than I normally would, because I just don’t trust my judgment right now.\n\nAnd there are a couple of board items I’ve put off, not because they’re impossible, but because I feel like if I make the call right now, I might make the wrong one. So yes, it is interfering some. Not to the point that everything has fallen apart, but enough that I notice I’m not doing my job with the same confidence or speed.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling especially worried or anxious lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say especially anxious. I mean, I do worry, but I’ve always been someone who thinks things through, maybe too much sometimes. Lately I notice myself turning things over in my head more, but I don’t know if that’s anxiety exactly or just me not feeling as steady as I usually do.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there panicking, if that’s what you mean. It’s more… I’ll pause and think, “Am I handling this right?” or “Should I check that again?” But I wouldn’t say I feel scared all the time or anything that strong. So I guess my answer is, maybe a little worried, but I’m not sure I’d call it especially anxious.\n\nDoctor: Before your husband retired, was there ever a time when you felt this same kind of low mood or loss of interest nearly every day for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: Yes… before he stopped working, I think there were times like that, though I’m not sure I was naming it that way at the time. It’s been kind of coming and going for maybe about a year now, these stretches where I feel low and sort of pulled back from things, not just for a day here or there.\n\nSome of those periods did last a couple of weeks, I think. It’s not always dramatic, so I can talk myself out of calling it anything serious, but looking back, yes, there were stretches where it was there nearly every day.\n\nThis latest one feels more connected to my husband not working anymore, like that change stirred it up or made it more noticeable. But the low patches themselves didn’t start only after that. They’ve been on and off before then.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that, because I haven’t been tracking it closely. I don’t think I’ve noticed a major problem, like something obvious where I’d say, “Oh, I’m not sleeping at all,” or anything like that.\n\nSome nights I might wake up or not feel completely rested, but I don’t know if that’s unusual for me or just normal at my age, or because I’m thinking about things before bed. I wouldn’t say sleep is the main thing I’ve been concerned about. If you mean trouble falling asleep every night or being up for hours, I don’t think so… at least not that I’ve really noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory?\n\nPatient: Memory… I don’t know if I’d call it memory exactly, like forgetting people’s names or not knowing where I am. It’s more that my head feels cloudy. That’s actually a lot of why I came in.\n\nI’ll be looking at a board packet, or a vendor email, something I used to go through pretty quickly, and I just sort of stare at it. I read the same part again and again, and then I still can’t seem to decide what to do with it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… both, actually, which sounds a little contradictory, but that’s what it feels like.

At home, especially when I’m just going from the bedroom to the kitchen or trying to get something done around the house, I feel slow. Like my legs are heavy, almost like I’m walking through mud. I’ll think, “Just get up and do the thing,” and then even moving feels like it takes more effort than it should.

But then when I’m in meetings, it’s almost the opposite on the outside. I’ll catch myself tapping my pen, shifting around in the chair, crossing and uncrossing my legs. I feel keyed up, like I can’t quite sit still, even if I’m trying to look calm and professional. So my body feels heavy sometimes, and other times it’s restless. My thinking feels slowed down too, like I said before, but the moving part is strange because it goes both ways depending on where I am.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I… yes, but not in the sense that I want to do something to myself. A few mornings, when I’ve first woken up, I’ve had this awful thought like, maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. And it scares me even saying that out loud, because I don’t want you to think I’m planning anything.

I don’t have a plan, and I don’t want to hurt myself. It’s more like a tired, heavy thought that comes over me for a minute, like not wanting to face the day. Then I feel guilty or frightened that I even thought it. So yes, the thought has been there a few times, but I’m not trying to harm myself.
